Earnings revision: We factor in Landmark 3Q26 revenues and revise up earnings by 5%/ 
1%/ 1%/ 1% in 2026-29E mainly on higher revenues of CW laser epi-wafer and diodes 
for 1.6T products, supported by rising demand from high-speed optical modules. We 
keep GM and Opex ratio largely unchanged.

Valuation: We employ a discounted P/E methodology (unchanged) and apply a 61.5x 
(unchanged, report link) target P/E multiple on our 2029E EPS, discounted back to 
2027E at a COE of 10.5% (beta 1.7x, risk free rate 1.6%, and market risk premium at 
5.1% - unchanged). Our target P/E multiple of 61.5x is derived from the PEG&M ratio of 
peers (unchanged) in the photonics supply chain. Consequently, our 12-month target 
price is now NT$4,459 (vs. previously at NT$4,401). Our new TP implied 2028E P/E of 
76x is between company’s avg. P/E at 62x and avg. +1 P/E at 91x. Maintain Buy.

### Page 3

Price Target Risks and Methodology - LandMark Optoelectronics Corp.  
Valuation: We employ a discounted P/E methodology, applying a 61.5x target P/E 
multiple on our 2029E EPS, then discount it back to 2027E at a COE of 10.5% (beta 1.7x, 
risk free rate 1.6% and market risk premium at 5.1%), to derive our 12-month target 
price of NT$4,459. Our target P/E multiple is based on the same PEG&M ratio as peers in 
the photonics supply chain. We are Buy-rated on LandMark. 
Key risk: Slower-than-expected SiPh penetration; stronger-than-expected competition; 
Integrated Device Manufacturers (IDMs) offering Epitaxial (EPI) wafers in-house.

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.
Coverage Universe: A list of all stocks in each coverage universe is available by primary analyst, stock and coverage universe at

https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html.
Not Rated (NR). The investment rating, target price and earnings estimates (where relevant) are removed pursuant to Goldman Sachs policy when
Goldman Sachs is acting in an advisory capacity in a merger or in a strategic transaction involving this company, when there are legal, regulatory or
policy constraints due to Goldman Sachs’ involvement in a transaction, and in certain other circumstances. Early-Stage Biotech (ES). An investment

rating and a target price are not assigned pursuant to Goldman Sachs policy when this company has neither a drug, treatment or medical device that
has passed a Phase II clinical trial nor a license to distribute a post-Phase II drug, treatment or medical device.  Rating Suspended (RS). Goldman Sachs
Research has suspended the investment rating and price target for this stock, because there is not a sufficient fundamental basis for determining an
investment rating or target price. The previous investment rating and target price, if any, are no longer in effect for this stock and should not be relied

upon.  Coverage Suspended (CS). Goldman Sachs has suspended coverage of this company.  Not Covered (NC). Goldman Sachs does not cover this
company.

公司研究報告 
本報告僅供宏遠投顧內部及客戶參考，雖已力求正確與完整，但因時間及市場客觀因素改變所造成產業、市場或個股之相關
條件改變，投資人需自行考量投資之實際狀況與風險，本公司恕不負擔任何法律責任及做任何保證。非經本公司同意，不得
將本報告加以引用或轉載。 
1 
6667 信紘科 (TRUSVAL)

報告日期： 2026/10/05            研究員：王蕎程 (Joe Wang) 
壹、訪 談／法 說 內 容 
重點摘要: 
 訂單滿載與高能見度：信紘科現有訂單達 230 億元，受惠於
AI 與半導體擴建潮，廠務設備與二次配工程訂單滿載，能見度
看至 2028 年。  
 大型統包與海外擴張：業務從單一工程升級至數十億級大型機
電統包，並結合策略夥伴進軍美、日、德、新等海外市場，帶
動營收成長。  
 綠色永續與持續收益：成立專責子公司銳源環境科技，聚焦廢
液處理與資源循環，將一次性工程建置延伸至維修與代操等持
續性收入。

一. 高科技建廠需求強勁，1H26 稅後 EPS 達 7.70 元 
信紘科以特氣特化廠務供應設備系統與二次配裝機工程為主要業
務，近年營運重心與服務範疇持續升級至二次配統包以及機電統
包工程。2025 年高科技產業營收達 59.45 億元，占整體營收高達 
93.56%；2026 年第二季高科技產業營收仍占絕大多數，半導體與
高科技建廠已成為公司最核心且穩定的營收來源

2026 年上半年營收達 32.19 億元；營業毛利 7.49 億元， 毛利率 
23.25% ；營業利益 3.55 億元，稅後淨利 3.79 億元，累計 EPS 達 
7.70 元。目前公司現有在手訂單高達 230 億元，訂 單能見度已直
達 2028 年，整體營運維持強勁成長動能。

二.半導體擴建為主要成長動能，工程與統包範疇擴大 
在廠務與工程服務部分，信紘科目前量產與工程實績包含特氣特
化廠務供應設備、系統建置及二次配管線裝機工程。為進一步提
升單案規模與營運效能，公司將服務領域延伸至二次配統包與大
型機電統包（GC/EPC），使專案金額邁入數十億級規模。後續業
務擴張重點，亦包含綠色製造與循環經濟，於 2025 年 12 月成
立專責子公司「銳源環境科技」，聚焦廢液處理與資源循環，將
一次性建置工程延伸至維修、營運代操及再生產品銷售等多元商
業模式等長期持續性收入。

目前主要服務據點皆配合客戶全球擴產需求建置，公司在台灣擁
有苗栗、台中、台南等據點，憑藉完整的本土供應鏈與人力調度，
能就近支援各大晶圓廠新建、擴建與改建之即時需求（主系統工

三. 預估 2026 / 2027 年稅後 EPS 14.76 / 16.83 元 
預估 2026 全年營收 71.17 億元，YoY +12.0%；營業毛利 16.41 
億元，YoY +27.1%，毛利率 23.06%；營業利益 7.92 億元，稅後
純益 7.57 億元，稅後 EPS 14.76 元。展望 2027 年，受惠全球
半導體廠與先進封裝建廠需求熱、在手 230 億元訂單高能見度，
加上大型 GC 統包工程持續認列與海外業務擴張，預計 2027 年
營收 78.29 億元，YoY +10.0%，營業毛利 18.18 億元，毛利率 
23.22% ，營業利益 9.76 億元，稅後純益 8.29 億元，稅後 EPS 為 
16.83 元。

四. 投資建議為「買進」，目標價 330 元 
綜合考量 (1)先進製程相關半導體需求維持強勁 (2) 公司可參與
的工程範圍擴大至海外及機電統包等大型工程，並觀察歷史及同
業本益比區間 ， 目前本益比 16 倍評價偏低 ， 故投資建議為「買進」，
目標價 330 元（20x 2027EPS）。

貳、產 業 及 營 運 概 況

一.公司簡介 
信紘科技（Trusval Technology，股票代號：6667）成立於 1995 年，
深耕高科技製造供應鏈布局，並由既有廠務供應設備系統服務，
逐步拓展至機電統包、建廠總承攬及整合型工程解決方案。目前
業務範疇涵蓋廠務設備、製程二次配裝機服務，以及高科技廠房
整合建置與機電統包工程，可依客戶建廠及擴產需求，提供從工
程規劃、設計、採購、施工、測試驗證至系統整合與專案管理的
一站式服務。憑藉跨工種整合、精密製程系統建置及大型專案管
理能力，公司持續提升高附加價值工程案件比重，協助客戶縮短
建廠時程、確保工程品質並提升營運效率。因應全球淨零轉型與
資源循環需求，信紘科技亦積極發展製程機能水設備、廢液處理
與循環經濟解方、資源零廢中心建置等業務，將工程整合能力延
伸至綠色製造與永續基礎建設。

台灣為公司最核心且成熟的基本盤，憑藉超過 30 年高科技產物
工程的深厚經驗，累積了卓越的技術能力與執行實力；得益於台
灣完整的高標準供應鏈以及敏捷的人力與材料調度，能高效支援
客戶新建、擴建及改建廠需求，加上工程需求穩定且執行週期較
短（主系統約 6 至 8 個月，二次配約 4 至 6 個月），構築了
公司長期穩健的營業基礎。

為打造未來成長新引擎，公司順應客戶海外建廠的全球布局與緊
湊時程，將成熟的工程能力輸出海外，信紘科不僅以既有產物工
程為切入點，逐步擴展至純水、廢水、無塵室及機電等領域以擴
大單一案件服務範疇，在非美日等新興區域更結合策略夥伴共同
承攬；同時，公司統包能力持續升級，從早期高技術門檻的二次
配工程，拓展至跨系統二次配統包，進而邁向承接大規模、高整
合度的跨國 GC 與工程管理角色，透過整合台外資源提升接單層
級與案件金額，全面貼近客戶全球建廠需求。

二.產業趨勢概況 
市場概況 
A.半導體產業概況  
半導體產業為全球數位經濟之核心基礎建設，近年在人工智慧
（AI）、高效能運算（HPC）及邊緣運算應用加速普及帶動下，產
業需求結構出現顯著變化。先進製程與高密度運算晶片需求持續
提升，使晶圓廠投資動能自庫存調整週期中快速復甦，並進入新
一輪資本支出擴張階段。 根據國際半導體產業協會（SEMI）2025
年發布之預測，全球 300mm 晶圓廠設備支出於 2026 年至 2028 年
間累計將達約 3,740 億美元，其中 2025 年單年度支出將首次突破
1,000 億美元水準。顯示全球半導體建廠投資已由短期循環性波
動，逐步轉為結構性成長態勢。另依 Deloitte（2025）預估，全球
半導體銷售額有機會於 2030 年前後突破 1 兆美元規模，反映產業
長期需求動能仍具成長潛力。

B.半導體產業 ESG 趨勢 
隨淨零排放與國際永續規範逐步落實，ESG 已由合規要求轉為半
導體供應鏈的重要競爭要素。依 SNS Insider（ 2025）預估，全球
半導體廢液處理市場將由 2024 年的 27.2 億美元成長至 2032 年的
49.2 億美元，其中亞太地區受惠於晶圓廠持續擴建與永續轉型，
成長動能可望高於全球平均。在此趨勢下，半導體廠務環保技術
逐步朝 「資源再生、能源轉化、源頭減量」三大方向發展。首先，
硫酸、氨氮廢水及有機溶劑等製程廢液，正由傳統末端處理轉向
回收再生，經純化後重新作為工業級或電子級原料使用，提高資
源循環率並降低廢棄物處置需求。其次，有機廢液處理逐步導入
Waste-to-Energy 技術，將廢液轉化為燃料或能源使用， 以降低外購
能源需求與碳排放。

此外，先進製程亦更加重視源頭減量，透過綠色化學品替代與先
進流體製程，降低強酸、強鹼及水資源使用量。隨 3nm 以下製程
持續發展，兼具降低化學品耗用、廢水負荷與營運成本效益的技
術，導入需求可望提升。 
整體而言，未來半導體廠務市場將由單純設備供應，逐步轉向整

Growth in multiples
We forecast revenue to more than double next year
Bloom Energy’s planned capacity expansion of up to 10GW strengthens Kaori’s
long-term SOFC growth outlook, while higher liquid cooling content and
shipments should sustain liquid cooling momentum into 2027. CSP CDU heat
exchangers and semiconductor equipment cooling could provide additional

growth drivers from 4Q26. We raise our 2027/28 EPS forecasts by 14%/12% and
increase our target price to NT$2,710 from NT$2,300. Reiterate HC O-PF rating.
Bloom Energy to double its capacity
Bloom Energy (BE) has announced plans to acquire a 158k sqft facility in F remont,
U
S, comparable in size to its existing Fremont manufacturing plant, which can
produce up to 5GW. We believe the acquisition could support an eventual doubling
of its fuel cell stack manufacturing capacity to 10GW. We expect its supply chain

to support shipments of 2/4/6GW in 2026/27/28 to meet strong solid oxide fuel
cell (SOFC). demand. To support this ramp, Kaori is expanding capacity to 6-7x its
current level by end-2027. We now forecast Kaori’s sales from BE to grow by
185%/66% YoY in 2027/28, versus our previous estimate of 101%/77%.
Strong liquid cooling momentum into 2027
With SpaceX planning an additional 6-8GW of datacentre capacity in 2027, we
b
elieve GPU server suppliers Supermicro and Dell will be key beneficiaries,

supporting continued growth across their supply chains from 2H26 into 2027.
Higher content value for Rubin manifolds, together with stronger CDU and rear-
door heat exchanger (RDHx) shipments, should support Kaori’s liquid cooling
momentum. We forecast its liquid cooling revenue to grow by 60%/25% YoY in
2027/28.
Diversifying the growth engines
While SOFC and Supermicro-related liquid cooling remain Kaori’s main growth
d
rivers, we believe plate heat exchangers for CSP CDUs and liquid cooling

applications in semiconductor equipment could emerge as additional growth drivers
from 4Q26. These opportunities should broaden Kaori’s customer and application
exposure, supporting its longer-term growth outlook.
Earnings revisions and valuation
We raise our 2027/28SP EPS forecasts by 14%/12% to reflect higher expected
d
emand and shipments for BE's fuel cells, alongside a stronger liquid cooling
outlook. We increase our TP to NT$2,710 from NT$2,300, based on an unchanged

40x 2H27-1H28SP PE. Reiterate High Conviction Outperform rating.

Investment thesis
Kaori is well positioned to capitalise on rising demand for liquid cooling, and it
expects related product shipments to ramp up. The fuel-cell business should be a
huge growth driver in 2026-27 to meet AI power infrastructure demand in the US.
In the longer term, we believe the company will also benefit from heat-pump
demand as Europe is phasing out gas boilers, despite some noise about near-term
demand. Kaori has been planning capacity expansion earlier than peers to fully

capture demand, with rising exposure to large clients. We thus expect the company
to enjoy robust earnings in coming years.

### Page 5

Growth in multiples Kaori - HC O-PF
6 October 2026 5
Catalysts
The major growth catalysts largely depend on fuel-cell projects for AI datacentres
taking off, increasing content value in liquid cooling offerings, and a clearer outlook
for hydrogen electrolyzer development.
Valuation details
We adopt a PE-based valuation methodology as we believe the market will mainly
focus on the earnings growth outlook, which is better reflected with PE metrics.

We base our valuation on 40x PE, which is around +1sd of its five-year average and
supported by an 93% EPS Cagr in 2026-28SP, to account for the surging demand
for AI server liquid cooling and power infrastructure. We are positive on the
company's long-term outlook given its growing stationary fuel cell demand from AI
datacentres, as well as its efforts to diversify customer exposure for liquid cooling
business. We believe the company can maintain its competitive edge over other

cooling players given its expertise in vacuum welding technology to manufacture
high-quality products.
Investment risks
Downside risks: 1) increased competition for Bloom Energy's fuel cell business or
new technology solutions replacing fuel cells; 2) slower kick-off of new fuel cell
projects for AI datacentres; 3) weaker-than-expected liquid cooling adoption; 4)
slower rollout of Europe/US government subsidies for heat pump installations.

### Page 1

(005930) 
삼성전자 
2026.09.29

반도체 손인준 
6154 / sij@eugenefn.com

긴 호흡으로 
 투자의견 STRONG BUY(유지) 
 목표주가 560,000원(유지) 
 현재주가 270,000원(9/28)

 삼성전자의 3Q26 영업이익이 103.6조원(+16%qoq)을 기록할 것으로 전
망한다. 메모리 가격 등 전반적인 반도체 업황에 대한 기존 가정은 대체로 
유지했으나, 최근 원화 강세에 따라 원/달러 환율 가정을 1,440원에서 
1,400원으로 낮추고 MX 사업부의 적자 확대를 반영해 실적 추정치를 하
향 조정했다. 또한 2Q26 재고가 3Q26 매출로 인식되는 과정에서 발생하
는 추가 성과급 충당금 부담도 반영했다.  
 DS 부문의 3Q26 영업이익은 104.0조원(+17%qoq)을 기록할 것으로 전
망한다. 서버 DRAM과 eSSD 중심의 가격 인상이 이어지며, DRAM과 
NAND의 Blended ASP는 각각 +17%, +18%QoQ 상승할 것으로 추정한
다. 1cnm 수율 개선과 HBM4의 빠른 램프업도 실적 개선에 기여할 전망이
다. Foundry/LSI는 HBM4 베이스다이 생산 확대, Groq 3 LPU 양산 및 선
단 공정 가격 인상에 힘입어 하반기 매출 성장이 예상된다. 다만 성과급 충
당금과 감가상각비 부담으로 영업적자 축소 폭은 제한적일 것으로 보인다. 
 DX 부문의 3Q26 영업이익은 -1.8조원(적자지속qoq)을 기록할 것으로 추
정한다. 삼성전자는 메모리 가격 급등에도 제품 가격 인상을 제한하며 시장 
점유율 확대에 주력하고 있다. 이러한 가격 정책과 갤럭시 Z 폴드8 등 신제
품 흥행에 힘입어 판매량은 당초 예상치를 상회할 것으로 보인다. 다만 원
가 상승분의 전가가 제한되면서 영업적자 폭은 크게 확대될 전망이다.  
 삼성전자의 4Q26 영업이익은 114.9조원(+11%qoq)을 기록할 것으로 전
망한다. DRAM과 NAND의 Blended ASP는 각각 +11%, +12%QoQ 상승
하며 견조한 흐름을 이어갈 것으로 보인다. 환율 가정 조정에 따라 2026년
과 2027년 영업이익 추정치를 각각 365.2조원(+735%yoy), 595.8조원
(+63%YoY)으로 낮췄다. 4Q26~1Q27 범용 DRAM·NAND 및 2027년 
HBM 가격 협상 환경은 공급업체에 유리하게 전개되고 있어, 향후 가격 가
정의 추가 상향 가능성이 높다고 판단한다.  
 극심한 DRAM 공급 부족으로 인한 HBM 적층 단수 하향 조정(base die 수
요 증가), Memory Wall에 대응하기 위한 3D Stacked DRAM·zHBM 도입 
논의 등은 파운드리의 기술적 가치를 부각시키는 요인이다. 파운드리의 수
익성 회복 속도는 완만할 전망이지만, 메모리와 파운드리를 연계한 턴키 비
즈니스의 시너지는 AI 반도체 시장 내 경쟁력을 높이고, 메모리 시장 점유
율 확대에도 기여할 것으로 보인다. 적극적인 메모리 가격 인상 정책으로 
업계 최고 수준의 Blended ASP도 장기간 유지할 것으로 예상한다. 
 이제 메모리 업종 주가는 업황의 지속 가능성을 확인하고 꾸준한 주주환원
을 반영하며, 중장기적으로 점진적인 상승세를 이어갈 것으로 판단한다. 삼
성전자는 메모리·파운드리를 아우르는 턴키 역량과 가격 정책에 기반한 실
적 차별화를 통해 피어 그룹 내 경쟁력을 높여갈 것으로 보인다.

Compliance Notice 
당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에도 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

### Page 1

美联储加息预期升温、 AI 融资成本攀升等多重压⼒叠加，本周全球科技股遭遇⼤规模抛售
—— 全球前 20 ⼤ AI 及半导体股市值已经合计蒸发逾 1 万亿美元，半导体 ETF （ SOXX ）创 11
年最⻓连跌纪录。周四， SK 海⼒⼠单⽇继续暴跌逾 10% ，英伟达、三星、软银⽆⼀幸免。
摘要：
加息预期、 AI 融资成本攀升与竞争格局恶化，多重压⼒在本周同步发酵，将全球科技股推⼊⼀场
⼤规模抛售。
周四全球科技股的抛售仍在继续，美股⽅⾯，英伟达当⽇收跌 1.84% ， AMD 跌 1.5% ，英特尔跌
约 2.8% ，戴尔科技重挫 6.8% 。亚洲市场跌幅更为惨烈， SK 海⼒⼠单⽇暴跌逾 10% ，三星电⼦下
跌 8.73% ，⽇本软银集团⼤跌 7.9% 。
据 CNBC 统计，截⾄本周四，全球市值最⾼的 20 只 AI 及半导体股票合计蒸发逾 1 万亿美元。追踪
芯⽚板块的 iShares 半导体 ETF （ SOXX ）周四录得连续第⼋个交易⽇下跌，区间累计跌幅约
12.7% ，据道琼斯市场数据，这是该 ETF 11 年来最⻓的连跌纪录。

英伟达 CEO ⻩仁勋在斯德哥尔摩出席活动时向彭博表⽰，他认为英伟达⽆需为 CoreWeave 提供
背书，并称 " 他们的资本成本实际上正在下降 " 。这番表态带动新云计算股（ neocloud ）盘中反
弹， CoreWeave 收涨 5.94% ， Nebius 上涨 1.29% 。
多重利空叠加，抛售逻辑层层递进
本轮抛售并⾮单⼀事件触发，⽽是多条负⾯线索在同⼀时间窗⼝内相互强化。
通胀数据是导⽕索。 周三公布的美国 9 ⽉消费者价格指数（ CPI ）⾼于预期，市场随即加⼤对美
联储进⼀步加息的押注。纳斯达克综合指数周三收跌 1.88% ⾄ 26692.87 点，标普 500 指数跌
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

1.11% ⾄ 7718.94 点。
Anthropic 数据令市场情绪雪上加霜。 据英国《⾦融时报》报道， AI 初创公司 Anthropic 在周三
⼀场私下投资者会议上披露，第三季度营收达 168 亿美元，环⽐增⻓约 50%—— 但这⼀增速远低
于第⼆季度约 145% 的环⽐增幅。 D.A. Davidson 分析师 Gil Luria 在客⼾报告中写道： " 市场似乎
将 Anthropic 的更新解读为负⾯信号，考虑到市场本就对 AI 交易⾼度敏感，这种反应可以理解。 "
Luria 本⼈对此持更为中性的看法。他指出， Anthropic 第三季度实现正向现⾦流，这表明该公司
和 OpenAI" 并⾮天⽣⽆法盈利的商业模式 " 。他还写道，从更宏观的 AI ⾏业来看， " 整体 AI 使⽤量
（以及 AI ⽀出）未必出现减速 " 。
中国竞争同步发酵。  ⻓鑫存储上周末宣布，在新架构上取得突破，可在更⼩空间内存储更多数
据。埃隆 · ⻢斯克随即在 X 平台发⽂称，中国将在 2028 年⽣产 " ⼤量内存芯⽚ " 。

与此同时，博通和英特尔的⾸席执⾏官本周⼀均警告称，融资问题可能成为 AI 扩张的重⼤阻
⼒，博通 CEO Hock Tan 表⽰，这⼀挑战甚⾄可能超过芯⽚或电⼒短缺。
利率上⾏，直接冲击 AI 基础设施投资回报
⾼利率环境对 AI 基础设施建设的冲击，是本轮抛售的核⼼逻辑之⼀。
投⾏ Post Oak Group 执⾏董事 James Vrachas 向 MarketWatch 表⽰，芯⽚股已经 " 消化了⼤量未
来增⻓预期 " ，股价本⾝已反映这些预期。在此背景下， " 情绪的轻微转变或对⽀出的质疑，都会
对股价产⽣显著冲击 " 。他说： " 相信 AI 的⻓期潜⼒，和相信每⼀家受益于这⼀增⻓的公司都被合
理定价，是两回事。 "
Baird 投资策略师 Ross Mayfield 向 MarketWatch 指出，国债收益率上⾏对芯⽚板块构成压⼒，尤
其是⾼成⻓股以及 "AI ⻛险谱系中更偏激进⼀端 " 、更依赖外部融资的公司。他表⽰， CPI 数据可
能加⼤美联储加息的紧迫性， " 这对短端利率造成巨⼤压⼒，对那些财务基础较弱、或持有与短

期利率挂钩的浮动利率债务的借款⼈打击尤为直接 " 。
Mayfield 还指出，即便是超⼤规模云服务商（ hyperscaler ）也并⾮对利率完全免疫。 " 即使是最
⼤、现⾦最充裕的公司，也必须重新评估这些项⽬的投资回报率， " 他说， " ⽽市场的⼤部分动能
都建⽴在超⼤规模云服务商对硬件和数据中⼼基础设施的⽀出之上，这就给整条供应链蒙上了
⼀层阴影。 "
亚欧市场跌幅更深，韩⽇股市⾸当其冲
美股的抛售情绪迅速蔓延⾄亚洲和欧洲市场，且跌幅普遍超过美股。
周四，韩国 SK 海⼒⼠单⽇暴跌逾 10% ，三星电⼦跌 8.73% ， LG Innotek 跌 8.95% ，⾸尔半导体
跌 9.28% ，三星 SDI 跌 6.26% 。韩国综合股价指数（ KOSPI ）逼近 4 ⽉以来最低收盘⽔平。

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
⽇本市场同样重挫。软银集团跌 7.9% ，半导体测试设备商爱德万测试（ Advantest ）暴跌近
14% ，芯⽚制造设备供应商东京电⼦跌 6.45% ，瑞萨电⼦跌 8% 。台积电跌 1.81% 。
欧洲⽅⾯，荷兰半导体设备巨头 ASML ⼩幅下跌，英⻜凌跌 5% ， ASM International 跌 7% 。
分析师：需求信号才是真正的警报线
尽管市场情绪低迷，部分分析师认为本轮抛售尚未触及 AI 需求的实质性恶化。
Yorkville Ives 科技分析师 Dan Ives 在报告中写道，本轮抛售反映的是市场对融资成本的担忧，⽽
⾮对 AI 算⼒需求的质疑。 " 美国国债收益率升破 5% ，确实推⾼了这些基础设施项⽬的融资成本，
投资者关注资产负债表和合同结构是合理的，但利率是融资问题，不是需求问题。 "

Ives 认为当前是买⼊机会，并表⽰ AI 基础设施热潮仍处于早期阶段。他明确列出了真正值得警惕
的信号： " 真正会改变我们判断的，是需求信号，⽽不是利率信号 "—— 包括超⼤规模云服务商削
减资本⽀出计划、合同取消或 GPU 租赁价格下跌。
Gabelli Funds 投资组合经理 Hendi Susanto 则向 MarketWatch 表⽰，许多芯⽚及半导体设备公
司 " 资产负债表依然稳健，⾃由现⾦流充裕 " ，他预计其中⼤多数公司 " 将以强劲态势收官今年 " ，
尤其是那些需求仍超过供给的公司。他还指出，即将到来的科技公司财报季将揭⽰ " 投资者在多
⼤程度上将通胀担忧纳⼊定价 " 。

Document 181: August CPI – Came in on our expectations (2026-10-15)

Source PDF: all report pdf/August CPI – Came in on our expectations (2026-10-15).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 16
Status: ok

### Page 1

#inflation #australia #rba #economicbulletinsaustralia #inflation
August CPI – Came in on our expectations
By Justin Smirk & Neha Sharma
15:27 September 30 2026 Share
August Monthly CPI: Headline 0.4%mth/4.0%yr, Trimmed Mean 0.2%mth/3.6%yr. Market
services ex volatile –0.4%mth/3.3%yr.

The CPI rose 0.4% in August, spot on Westpac’s expectation but a bit softer than market
expectations of 0.5%. Due to base effects the annual pace picked up to 4.0%yr from 3.5%yr.

### Page 2

Key factors driving the headline result were auto fuels (14.8%); games, toys & hobbies (10.2%) with
rents providing a solid base (0.3%) while some offset was provided by both domestic (–4.9%) and
international holiday travel & accommodation (–1.5%).
Trimmed mean (TM) inflation printed 0.2% again bang on Westpac’s expectation while the market
was slightly higher at 0.3%. The annual pace was flat at 3.6%yr, the third month in a row at this pace,
while the six-month annualise pace was steady at 3.9%yr.

Key factors driving the TM print were: rents (0.3%) and new dwelling purchases (0.2%). The TM
distribution ranged from –0.38% to 0.82%.
Today’s inflation update revealed price discounting for some durable goods as retail demand softens.
We would expect to see a bounce from these categories in the following month. There was also
offsetting strength elsewhere in durable good so at least for now, the momentum continues to
suggest some near term upside risk to our current inflation estimates.

Nevertheless, as the TM came in on expectations and based on a first quick pass at updating the
quarterly profile, we see little pressure to revise our current Q3 estimates of 1.3%qtr for the CPI and
0.9%qtr for the TM.
The RBA remains alert to near term inflationary risks. We are expecting the momentum in TM
inflation to remain well above the top of the target band through to year-end underpinning our
expectations for the RBA to raise rates again in November.

### Page 3

August CPI in more detail
Following the bumper read in July, the monthly CPI moderated in August rising 0.4%mth. This was in
line with our expectations and slightly softer than the market consensus. This took the year-ended pace
to 4.0%yr. On a seasonally adjusted basis the CPI rose 0.7%mth highlighting the impact of seasonal
sales in August.

They key drivers behind the monthly lift were automotive fuel, up 14.8%mth, contributing 0.5ppts to the
headline figure. The surge resulted from the ending of the fuel excise discount on August 3 and rising
global energy prices. With crude oil prices still elevated due to the conflict in the middle east, and
growing shortages of refined products due to both the middle east conflict and Ukrainian assaults on
Russian refining capacity, auto fuel is expected to remain an inflationary impulse at least until the end
of 2026.

The monthly TM also softened, rising 0.2%mth compared to the 0.5%mth read from July. The result
was again in line with our expectations, and very close at two decimal places (0.24%mth vs
0.23%mth), but softer than the market's view. The year-ended pace held at 3.6%yr for the third
consecutive month, although it continued to ease marginally at the second decimal place. In contrast,
the six-month annualised pace has continued to trend higher since April and is currently running at
3.9%yr.

### Page 4

There is a possibility that as the ABS continues to grapple with seasonal adjustment issues we will see
revisions to the TM estimates. Last July also saw a strong 0.48%mth read which eased to 0.29%mth in
August. This is not too dissimilar from the 0.48%mth and 0.24%mth reads for 2026.

We are expecting core inflation to start to moderate as we get to year end but our current estimate for
the month of December still has the TM running at a 3.3% in the year while the six-month annualised
pace is still a chunky 3.1%. Applying a trim on the seasonally adjusted 3m/3m expenditure classes
yields a TM of 0.9%qtr currently. This is in line with our current Q3 TM estimate.

Other measures of underlying inflation told a similar story, easing on a monthly basis but holding steady
on a year-ended basis. Market services ex volatiles fell –0.4%mth to be 3.3%yr higher. Excluding both
volatiles & holidays saw a 0.2%mth/4.1%yr lift.

Most market services components eased in the month (including on a seasonally adjusted basis) but
annual growth across this group as a whole remained above the RBA's 2-3% target band. The near-
term momentum (three-month annualised) is also quite strong across several components of services.
Of note include deposit and loan facilities (11.2%mth), sports participation (10.9%), clothing repair
(7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other household services (5.2%). We are closely watching market
services as this is where inflationary risks, outside of energy, appear to be most persistent.

Due to its sizable weight in the CPI, and the fact price changes here tend to be less volatile than many
other categories, so they almost always appear in the TM estimates, housing prices remain a key
focus. Rents rose 0.3%mth in August, in line with our expectations, to be up 3.6%yr. Since April, the
seasonally adjusted rents series has been running lower than the non-seasonally adjusted series. We
are expecting rents to hold around this pace till year end before moderating modestly as we move
through 2027.

New dwellings inflation was 0.2%mth in August (softer than our expectation for a 0.4%mth rise). Since
peaking in May, monthly price growth has slowed in each successive month, potentially reflecting
increasing difficulty for developers in passing through higher costs amid softer conditions in established
housing markets and weaker demand in the face of higher interest rates. The annual pace looks to
have topped out. It was at 5.8%yr in June easing to 5.4%yr currently with the three-month annualised
pace at 3.7%. We expect dwelling price inflation to continue easing through to the end of 2027 as
falling established house prices limit the ability of builders to pass on rising construction costs. There is
a medium-term risk that as conditions improve builders may raise prices more aggressively to claw
back margins.

### Page 5

Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated in August. The share of the basket running above 3%yr
eased from 45% to 42%, with the share below 2%yr increasing from 38% to 41%.

### Page 6

Key surprises in the month:
Upside surprises

Downside surprises
Insurance surprised to the upside (0.7%mth vs 0.3%mth) after a run of weaker-than-usual reads over
the past two months.
Motor vehicle repair costs rose a solid 0.8% against our 0.3%mth expectations, with spare parts and
accessories also up 0.3%mth vs –0.4%mth.
Footwear rose 1.7%mth against our expectations for a -2.9%mth decline, the gains were broad-
based across women, men & children.
Household goods surprised to the upside. Household appliances, utensils and tools rose 1.9%mth
against our flat expectations, meanwhile household textiles fell –1.0%mth against our expectations
for a –5.0%mth fall. Furniture and furnishings also fell by less than expected –0.6%mth vs –1.3%mth.
Recreation and culture surprised to the upside (–1.0%mth vs –1.5%mth), owing to a 1.4%mth lift in
other recreation, sport & culture against our expectations for a flat read on the back of an outsized
gain in games, toys & hobbies (10.2%mth vs 2.5%mth). Holiday travel was marginally less weak than
expected (–3.3% vs –3.5%mth).

### Page 7

Food was the key downside miss falling –0.1%mth against our expectations for a 0.2%mth lift. Meat
& seafood (–0.9%mth vs 0.5%mth) and fruit & vegetables (–1.2%mth vs 0.1%mth) were the key
areas of weakness.
Audio, visual and computing equipment and services came in weaker-than-expected (0.7%mth vs
1.2%mth), owing to a softer 0.6%mth lift in computing services (3.0%mth expected). This was partly
balanced by a stronger than expected lift in computing equipment (0.9%mth vs –0.5%mth). The latter

is where we are most likely to see the impact of the AI boom in the CPI, due to computer chip
inflation, other than construction cost inflation for new dwellings.
Motor vehicles came in lower at –0.4%mth vs –0.1%mth which could be the start of price pressures
associated with the rapid expansion of the share of Chinese cars in the Australian market.
Automotive fuel was also slightly softer at 14.8%mth vs 16.7%mth.
Garments and accessories were both softer. Garments fell –1.9%mth against our expectations for a

0.2%mth rise, meanwhile accessories came in at 0.1%mth vs 0.5%mth expected.

### Page 8

What does this mean for the RBA?
Yesterday, post the RBA rate hike and monetary policy decision statement, Westpac changed its view
from a November follow up rate hike being a risk to being the base case. The post-meeting statement
was notable for its emphasis on the Middle East conflict, noting that “global energy prices are now
much higher than had been assumed in the August forecasts”. In addition, RBA’s liaison suggests firms
are passing this on or planning to do so. The pass-through was characterised as partial, and additional

to domestically driven inflation arising from capacity pressures. We read this as implying that the RBA
is concerned about upside risks to underlying inflation in Q4 of this year, as well as Q3.

### Page 9

Related articles
Our current forecast of 0.9%qtr for the September quarter TM stands post today’s release and
represents a step up from 0.8% in the June quarter holding the annual pace at 3.6%yr. Our current
forecast for the Q4 TM is 0.7%qtr/3.3%yr but given the move in energy prices, and anecdotes of firms
now passing on those rising costs, we too see upside risks to this estimate.

As such, we hold to the view that the RBA will follow up with another rate hike in November. For more
information on the RBA decision please see Luci Ellis’s note “RBA hikes in September, November
follow-up now expected".

Document 260: First Impressions_ NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion, September quarter 2026 (2026-10-16)

Source PDF: all report pdf/First Impressions_ NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion, September quarter 2026 (2026-10-16).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

#businessconditions #newzealand
First Impressions: NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion,
September quarter 2026
By Michael Gordon
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
08:35 October 06 2026 Share
Businesses are feeling more optimistic about the months ahead, but current conditions
remain tough for many industries. Cost pressures eased off a little compared to last quarter.
Key results (seasonally adjusted)
General business situation: +40 (Prev: +14)

### Page 2

Trading activity, past three months: -1 (Prev: +1) 
Trading activity, next three months: +15 (Prev: +11) 
Average selling prices, past three months: +27 (Prev: +37) 
Average selling prices, next three months: +45 (Prev: +51)

The NZIER’s quarterly survey of business opinion showed a strong lift in general confidence in
September, despite resurgent fuel prices and the uncertainty around the looming election. Firms were
more confident about their prospects for the next few months, even though their recent performance
remained rough in many industries. 
The survey was held over a relatively compressed period (9 to 28 September), with around 40% of
responses coming back on the first day. Later responses were less confident, as the resurgence in
world oil prices was finally reflected in prices at the pump over the course of the month. 
General business sentiment rose to a net 40% positive compared to a net 14% in June, returning to
around the levels seen in the December quarter last year (before the Middle East conflict). However,
the own-activity measures, which tend to have a closer correspondence with GDP growth, were more
subdued. Trading activity over the last three months was slightly weaker than in June, while
expectations for the quarter ahead saw a more modest four-point increase to +15%. 
Other forward-looking measures were also up for the quarter. Hiring intentions rose strongly to a net
23% positive (although past employment was down). Notably, firms continue to report that workers are
becoming harder to find, despite the ongoing rise in unemployment over the last couple of years. That
said, the proportion of firms citing labour as a factor limiting output declined this quarter. Investment
intentions picked up, more so for plant and machinery than for buildings. 
The results were mixed across industries. Manufacturing and services were more upbeat for the
months ahead but reported substantially weaker performance over the last quarter. In contrast, builders
and merchants reported both stronger past results and greater optimism about the near-term outlook. 
The pricing measures in the survey were down compared to June – not entirely surprising as fuel
prices were lower on average through the September quarter compared to the June quarter. The
notable exception was merchants, who expected both higher costs and prices for the quarter ahead. 
The implications for the RBNZ ahead of its 28 October policy review are unclear – there is certainly no
smoking gun in this report that would demand that the RBNZ abandon the “pause” strategy
communicated at the September MPS. Optimism among businesses remains high despite the obvious
headwinds, but there is still a wide gap between hopes and reality. The pricing measures have eased
compared to last quarter, but that may simply confirm the RBNZ’s expectation that annual inflation will
drop from the 4.1% recorded last quarter as fuel prices eased. The September quarter CPI, released
on 22 October, may be more informative in that respect. 
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist
P: +64 21 749 506

Same fears, different crisis

### Page 2

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

2 
Same fears, different crisis 
France's political and fiscal challenges have sparked comparisons with the eurozone 
debt crisis. But while some warning signs look familiar, key differences mean today's 
France is not Greece in 2010 and fears of a new euro crisis are overblown 
History hardly ever repeats. It rhymes. More than 16 years after the eurozone sovereign 
debt crisis, France's political and fiscal troubles have revived memories of that period. 
However, as is so often the case with memories, particularly those of experts and 
market participants who weren’t even around at that time, they tend to be blurry and 
oversimplified. Luckily, yours truly and other members of ING’s Research team were 
around at that time and can call themselves true euro crisis veterans. 
A trip back to the euro crisis 
Let me take you on a trip down memory lane. The euro crisis began with a revision, not 
a recession. In October 2009, the newly elected PASOK government of George 
Papandreou announced that the 2009 budget deficit would be around 12.7% of GDP, 
roughly double what Athens had previously reported. Eurostat later put the final figure 
at 15.4%. Overnight, the problem was no longer the size of the hole; it was that nobody 
had known how deep it was. Suspicion that Greece had fought its way into eurozone 
membership with creative accounting accompanied the entire euro crisis. 
After the statistical revelation, the numbers were brutal: public debt was close to 130% 
of GDP and heading for 150%. The current account deficit had hit around 15% of GDP in 
2008: Greece was borrowing abroad not only to fund its state but its entire economy. 
Unit labour costs in Greece had risen much faster than in Germany since euro entry. 
Interestingly, markets did not panic at once. When the revision came out, the 10-year 
Greek spread over Bunds was around 130bp, about where France trades now. It took six 
months of failed promises, rating downgrades and EU dithering to turn that into a 
funding crisis. 
Shortly before Greece finally received a European bailout, and the infamous troika of the 
European Commission, the ECB and IMF basically took over policymaking in Greece, the 
spread between Greek and German debt was above 900bp. And actually it was above 
3000bp two years later, at the time of the second bailout and haircuts on Greek debt. 
It didn’t take long before the debt crisis spread from Greece to other European capitals. 
Not at once but over two years, with spreads vis-à-vis Germany reaching around 1500bp 
in the case of Portugal, 1100bp in the case of Ireland and around 600bp for Spain and 
Italy. The transmission channels of contagion differed. While the bailout of its banks 
blew up the Irish budget deficit to 32% of GDP, Spain saw its real estate market 
imploding with regional banks going bust, Portugal suffered from an enormous current 
account deficit and high private debt and, last but not least, Italy got into trouble due to 
high government debt, low growth and a lack of political credibility. What these 
countries had in common was a lack of market confidence in the existence of a credible 
lender of last resort and the unresolved doom loop between banks and sovereigns. 
It took Mario Draghi’s ‘whatever it takes’, a multi-layered firewall of new European 
(rescue) institutions, as well as the political will in European capitals to implement 
austerity measures and structural reforms, to stop the crisis.

### Page 3

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

3 
Why France is not Greece 
We receive many questions about whether France is the new Greece and whether we 
are stumbling into the next euro debt crisis. And, indeed, at least at first glance, some 
numbers are worrying: France's debt ratio is now similar to that of Italy in 2011 and 
Greece in 2009. The French fiscal deficit is wider than Italy's was then. The primary 
deficit – the gap before interest – is close to 3% of GDP. Closing it requires a political 
consensus that France has not produced since the 1970s. Most worrying is the French 
track record of missing its own and European targets for years, if not decades. Greece's 
credibility was destroyed by false statistics; France's is being eroded by false promises 
and the inability or unwillingness to start fiscal and structural reforms. 
While these elements are clearly frightening, there are also significant differences 
between France now and Greece back then. While Greece had a substantial current 
account deficit, France’s current account is basically in balance, the household savings 
rate is at around 18% and the effective interest rate France pays on its whole debt stock 
is still below nominal GDP growth. Greece in 2010 paid around 5% on its debt stock while 
the economy was shrinking. France has a fiscal problem but not a funding problem. 
What's more, the eurozone now has a firewall that it didn’t have 16 years ago. 
Admittedly, the firewall has been constructed for smaller eurozone countries, not for 
France or Italy. 
All of this means that fears about a new euro debt crisis are overdone. Fears about the 
political ability and willingness to bring national economies and the entire EU on course 
for reforms and sustainable public finances, unfortunately, are not.

Watch: Debt crisis 2.0? Why France isn't the new Greece

carsten.brzeski@ing.de

### Page 4

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

4 
Our key calls this month 
• Energy prices: Despite growing tensions, we still expect oil prices to fall materially 
into year-end. We’re sticking to our forecast for Brent crude to average $80/bbl 
through the fourth quarter. European gas is the bigger near-term risk; we expect 
Dutch TTF to average €75/MWh this quarter before falling sharply from 2Q27 as 
supply normalises. 
• United States: We expect the Fed to hike rates again in December, but we don’t 
expect further increases in 2027 as inflation pressure begins to fade. 
• Eurozone: Growth has proved resilient but higher energy costs, rising bond yields and 
French fiscal uncertainty should slow growth through winter. We expect one more 
ECB hike in December, taking the deposit rate to 2.75%. 
• China: The recent State Council meeting appeared to signal a more proactive policy 
push amid flagging growth. Should economic momentum remain subdued into year-
end, policymakers may respond with further fiscal stimulus and a broader easing of 
monetary policy. 
• United Kingdom: Inflation looks set to briefly top 4% next year and that's likely to 
trigger a November insurance rate hike from the Bank of England. We tentatively 
expect that to be followed by another hike in February. 
• Market rates: The balance of probabilities suggests there is at least one more leg of 
long-end weakness still to play out. This could take the US 10-year yield back into 
the 5.25% to 5.5% range. For later in 2027, we think inflation will come down, and 
long rates should too. 
• FX: We see limited scope for a near-term EUR/USD rebound. Key factors for EUR/USD 
now are a hawkish Fed and a re-pricing of the ECB cycle on French fiscal concerns. 
Neither of those factors appears set for an imminent reversal, leaving EUR/USD to 
languish in a new 1.10-1.15 range.

Oil prices are holding firm despite Persian Gulf supply increasing

Persian Gulf oil flows pick-up 
There are few signs of de-escalation in the Middle East. If anything, it appears as though 
the conflict is broadening, with the Houthis in Yemen increasingly targeting Saudi 
Arabia. Despite little improvement in the geopolitical environment, oil flows from the 
Persian Gulf have been trending higher with supply from the region returning to 70-75% 
of pre-war levels, although there are some estimates suggesting we could be back to as 
high as 80-90% of pre-war flows. 
The fact that oil prices are well-supported despite the improvement in flows from the 
region appears to reflect concerns over how sustainable this supply is, particularly when 
considering the geopolitical backdrop. The market continues to price in a large risk 
premium in the absence of a deal between the US and Iran. And to see this risk premium 
erode, the market may need the comfort of a deal. Failing that, time may be enough for 
oil prices to trend lower, assuming supplies from the region continue to hold up. For now, 
we are holding onto our 4Q26 Brent forecast of $80/bbl and FY27 forecast of $71/bbl. 
However, given the high level of uncertainty, this is a low-conviction call, specifically for 
4Q26. 
As oil flows from the Persian Gulf have picked up, another bottleneck has become 
increasingly noticeable in the oil market. There is significant tightness in the tanker 
market, which has seen tanker freight rates surge to record levels. Oil flows from the 
Persian Gulf have become increasingly inefficient due to the challenging environment, 
which has absorbed more of the global tanker fleet. And the only way this starts to 
normalise is if the way oil flows from the region also starts to normalise. 
The other pain point for the oil market remains diesel, which will be a concern as we 
head further into the Northern Hemisphere winter. Disruptions from the Persian Gulf and 
Russia have tightened the market, while the risk of export restrictions from the US has 
kept the market nervous. The risk of US export restrictions appears to have subsided, 
Warren Patterson 
Head of Commodities Strategy 
warren.patterson@ing.com

Oil prices hold firm despite Persian 
Gulf supply growing  
Oil prices remain firm despite rising supply from the Persian Gulf. We expect oil prices to 
trade lower into year-end. More positive signs of a potential US-Iran deal would give us 
more confidence in this view. Meanwhile, European gas looks vulnerable heading deeper 
into the heating season

### Page 7

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

7 
with Europe set to release diesel from strategic reserves. Furthermore, expensive freight 
suggests that refined product prices are likely to remain elevated. 
Natural gas vulnerable heading into heating season 
European natural gas prices have traded at their highest level since December 2022, 
with supply concerns growing as the region heads into the heating season. 
Unfortunately for the natural gas market, we have not seen a similar recovery in LNG 
flows from the Persian Gulf as we have with oil. Therefore, LNG flows from the region 
remain down about 80% YoY. The market is increasingly accepting that Persian Gulf LNG 
could remain largely absent from global markets for much of the Northern Hemisphere 
winter. This means Asia and Europe will continue competing for spot cargoes throughout 
the winter, keeping prices well supported and leaving the market vulnerable to further 
spikes. 
European gas storage remains a concern, with it a little under 75% full, well below the 
five-year average of 89%. Low storage has seen the German government instruct state-
owned SEFE to buy and store an additional 8TWh of natural gas by 15 December. The 
prospect of essentially forced buying provides further support to the market. 
With little sign of an imminent recovery in Persian Gulf LNG supply, weather will be 
crucial this winter, and there is growing expectation that Europe will experience a winter 
of milder temperatures due to El Niño, which would provide some relief to the market. 
However, it is not a strong relationship, and El Niño does not guarantee milder 
temperatures. The North Asia winter will also be important for the LNG market, as El 
Niño can also typically mean milder temperatures in this region. 
We now expect TTF to average EUR73/MWh over the 26/27 winter, up from a previous 
forecast of EUR65/MWh. The market will likely be very volatile over this period, given the 
lower starting point for gas storage. Prices are then expected to sell off aggressively 
from 2Q27 as Persian Gulf supply normalises, and we see a further ramp-up in US LNG 
export capacity.

10

Our latest views on the major central 
banks 
Our take on what could be next for the Federal Reserve, the European Central Bank, the 
Bank of England and the Bank of Japan over the coming months

### Page 11

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

11 
intervention. For that, the French government would first have to implement austerity 
measures before the ECB would ever consider starting any bond purchases. 
However, beyond the question of restarting bond purchases, the rise in bond yields has 
already impacted eurozone growth and inflation. In fact, the surge in German bond 
yields since the September meeting has already had roughly the same impact on 
growth and inflation as an additional 25bp rate hike. 
For France, it's closer to a 50bp hike. With bond markets doing the ECB's job, some 
central bank officials might be less keen to continue hiking than they were at the 
September meeting. 
Bank of England 
We now expect two rate hikes from the Bank of England – in November and February. 
Until now, the Bank has cast its decision not to cut rates, as implicitly planned before the 
Iran War, as de facto tightening. But recent comments suggest it increasingly feels it 
needs to act. 
The Bank’s November inflation forecasts are likely to show CPI briefly above 4%, and that 
is likely to be the catalyst for a hike, citing previous research that inflation at these levels 
is historically more likely to trigger second-round effects. 
But the case for hikes is still not compelling. With the jobs market still relatively cool, 
we’re sceptical that wage growth is likely to react to higher energy prices anywhere 
near as much as it did four years ago. Interest rates are also more evidently restrictive 
than they are in the eurozone or the US. That still points to rate cuts from late 2027 and 
into 2028. 
Bank of Japan 
The market prices in very low chances of a back-to-back BoJ hike on 30 October 
following its 25bp hike to 1.25% last month. That move saw two dissenters, and the BoJ 
typically takes time to assess the impact of policy changes before further adjustments. 
Nonetheless, the central bank continues to see upside risks to inflation and seems intent 
on a terminal policy rate somewhere in the 1.75-2.00% area next year. 
Most in the market are expecting the next hike to come in December, seeing the BoJ 
switching to a quarterly cycle from a six-monthly cycle of tightening policy. That faster 
pace of tightening would seemingly be in accord with Washington's desire for BoJ 
monetary policy to provide some credible macro support to July's joint US-Japan 
intervention in the yen. 
We see risks that the next hike could be pushed back into January, however. This allows 
the BoJ a little more time to monitor the economy. Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi's 
government remains set on a domestic demand push, and we have a slight preference 
that the next two hikes are backloaded into January and April, right before the 
consumption tax rate on food is cut to 1% from 8%.

### Page 12

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

President Donald Trump’s approval rating has hit new lows as we approach the US midterms in November

The US midterm elections are on 3 November, with all 435 House of Representatives 
seats and 35 of the 100 Senate seats up for election. Worries about the cost of living and 
the impact of AI on job security are regularly cited as key factors driving sentiment, 
while support for the conflict with Iran has wavered. Opinion polls currently suggest the 
Democrats lead by around eight percentage points nationally, and President Donald 
Trump’s own approval rating has hit new lows, leading to Republican Party concerns 
about a demotivated electoral base. 
Pollsters and prediction markets anticipate a Democrat takeover of Congress, but 
surprises do happen in US politics. While the midterms rarely lead to radical changes in 
policy direction, the result could significantly constrain President Trump, putting more 
focus on foreign and trade policy. We look at what the possible outcomes could mean. 
Midterm election scenarios

Source: ING

US midterm elections: 3 scenarios for 
markets  
Intensifying cost-of-living concerns, linked to the price of motor fuel and the war with 
Iran, have boosted the Democrats' chances of regaining control of Congress, but nothing 
is guaranteed in US elections. We look at the three possible scenarios and what they 
may mean for the economy and markets

### Page 13

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

13 
Scenario 1: A Republican revival 
Republicans retain control of both the House and the Senate 
This is seemingly the least likely outcome at the current juncture, given the state of the 
opinion polls. Nonetheless, if it happens, President Trump will see it as justification for his 
America First strategy. With re-election no longer a consideration, Trump’s final two 
years are likely to focus on his legacy, entrenching what he views as his defining 
achievements. 
We are looking at a further strengthening of border enforcement with expanded 
deportations. Tariff policy is likely to be increasingly focused on incentivising foreign 
partners to align with US international policy, together with more defence spending 
demanded of NATO members. Trump will also feel emboldened to push hard 
economically and militarily against Iran with demands that other countries contribute to 
those efforts. Deregulation is likely to be a theme, while fast-tracking AI/technology 
innovation and incentivising manufacturing and energy production (both fossil and 
nuclear) will be the main policies aimed at boosting economic growth. 
Fiscal worries are likely to intensify, though. While few expect the president to follow 
through with his promise of $5,000 checks for returning Republicans to Congress, more 
defence spending will not be fully offset by efficiency savings or lower healthcare 
expenditure. Fiscal sustainability fears will intensify. At the same time, an escalation of 
military action in the Middle East could prompt significant energy market disruption, 
resulting in higher prices. Should the president force through “Trump dividend” 
payments, the Federal Reserve will come under even more market pressure to respond 
with higher interest rates, raising fears of a boom-bust cycle. 
The market response to this outcome would be upward pressure on bond yields right 
along the curve. The front end reflects an intensification in the rate hike risk. The back 
end is spooked by the prospect of a further widening in the fiscal deficit and reactive 
tightening by the Fed. The 10yr yield likely pops above 5.5% and stays above, while the 
2yr yield rises too and holds at around 5%. There is a contrarian possibility here that an 
emboldened Republican Party decides to act on the fiscal deficit, ultimately helping drive 
bond yields back down. But it’s more likely that such an action would require a bad pain 
trade first for Treasuries to trigger such action. 
Cyclically, this would be the most positive for the dollar on the back of the looser fiscal, 
tighter monetary policy outcome. Higher bond yields would continue to pressure-test 
the global financial system, with emerging market currencies and perhaps the euro too, 
via French government bonds, coming under further pressure. 
Scenario 2: Trump constrained 
Democrats win control of the House, Republicans retain the Senate 
The House, where officials serve two-year terms, is currently split between 219 
Republicans and 214 Democrats, with 2 seats vacant. Pollsters and prediction markets 
appear confident the Democrats will win here with relative ease. The Senate, currently 
split between 53 Republicans and 45 Democrats with 2 independents who vote with the 
Democrats, is a much closer call. The Democrats need to flip 4 of the 22 Republican 
seats and hold on to all 13 of their seats that are up for election to gain control of the 
Senate. 
Even if the Democrats fail to win the Senate, they will stifle President Trump’s legislative 
agenda over his final two years in power. Spending and tax proposals will be gridlocked 
while many Democrats will use the House victory to justify stalling data centre 
construction in their constituencies. There will be more scrutiny of technology 
companies and AI development, with hearings before Congress being demanded. Fossil 
fuel producers may face similar challenges.

### Page 14

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

14 
A key question is whether a constrained President Trump, entering the final phase of his 
tenure, becomes more willing to accept political or market disruption in pursuit of his 
objectives. Given the gridlock in Congress, the president will only be able to implement 
policy where he can use Executive Powers: 1) Foreign policy and diplomacy, including 
trade policy, 2) national security and defence, 3) law enforcement and regulatory policy, 
4) appointments of personnel, and 5) administration and management policy. This 
suggests Iran and tariffs/trade policy are likely to be the focus for the president, but the 
Democrats could use Congressional power to try and restrain his actions. 
Fiscal dynamics will remain a concern, although hitting the debt ceiling could act as a 
temporary brake. It will likely be an issue by mid-summer 2027 with little ground for 
compromise between the two sides in Congress. This risks an extended government 
shutdown, potentially exceeding last October’s shutdown, which depressed GDP growth 
in 4Q 2025 by a full percentage point. The Federal Reserve risks “flying blind” again with 
no government economic statistics being collated or released. 
It is the House where impeachment proceedings are started and a new round of action 
is possible. To be impeached, 67 of 100 Senators must vote in favour, which is likely not 
going to happen given there won’t be enough Democrats (and supportive Republicans) 
to force it through. Nonetheless, the Democrats could use it as a way to constrain the 
president, but it risks toxifying the political situation in the US. Rather than the president 
facing such action, it has been mooted that the Democrats could launch investigations 
into members of his cabinet. 
This outcome is broadly in line with the central market discount. The Democrats have 
one foot in the door, but remain broadly powerless to make meaningful change. Policy 
stasis leaves the bond market to balance the trade-off between a still elevated fiscal 
deficit and perhaps some taming of inflation concerns. We will probably see front-end 
yields easing lower while back-end yields maintain an upside bias, albeit within our 
preferred 5.25% to 5.5% range. 
The dollar broadly prices in this scenario and a split Congress probably will not be 
significant enough to drag attention away from elevated inflation and a Fed intent on 
delivering at least one more rate hike. A prolonged government shutdown could start to 
weigh on the dollar in the second half of 2027, however. 
Scenario 3: Democrat clean sweep 
Democrats win control of both the House and the Senate 
Given the polling and President Trump’s poor personal approval ratings, this is the most 
likely outcome priced in prediction markets, although political analysts remain on the 
fence between this and scenario 2. 
The implications are similar to scenario 2, but Democrats would likely feel even more 
confident about pushing back against President Trump’s policies on Iran, support of 
Israel and any escalation of tariffs. They will be applying pressure on the president to 
sound tougher towards Russia and to provide support to Ukraine. Domestically, 
Democrats in Congress will try and pass legislation that improves healthcare access in 
return for some acquiescence surrounding the president’s demands for higher defence 
spending – a government shutdown could reflect badly on the Democrats if they have 
control of both the House and the Senate. Fiscal concerns will persist. 
A very strong showing for the Democrats that leads to a sizeable majority would 
heighten the chances of a third impeachment of President Trump, although it would 
need a sweeping victory to offer any chance of a conviction. 
More fundamentally, a heavy defeat in the House and Senate could prompt figures 
within the Republican Party to take the view that it is time to open the debate on its

### Page 15

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

15 
future direction. With President Trump’s influence set to fade, leading lights within the 
party, most notably JD Vance and Marco Rubio, may attempt to shore up support for 
their own campaigns as they look to the 2028 presidential election. Similarly, a large 
Democrat victory will intensify speculation over whether the party’s candidate should be 
a traditionalist or whether the Left have the momentum to provide the 2028 candidate. 
This outcome is not dramatically different from the previous scenario. Here the 
Democrats have a mandate to secure some change, but will remain constrained by an 
obstructive executive branch. Front-end yields come under upside pressure. Back-end 
yields possibly see a silver lining from more tariffs acting as a tax hike, but at the same 
time pressured by inflation. The 10yr risks getting towards 5.5%, but without breaking 
above. 
The dollar could see a little weakness here as an emboldened Democratic Party takes 
greater issue with Republican policy, including deregulation and the AI build-out. 
What it means for Europe and China 
Should Republicans pull off a huge surprise and retain control of Congress, political 
pressure on Europe will increase. The risk of a forced deal between Russia and Ukraine, 
to the disadvantage of Ukraine and Europe, increases and could undermine economic 
sentiment in Europe. A new escalation in the Middle East would push up energy prices 
and harm the European economy. Europe’s latest shift towards more protectionist trade 
policies could bring new global trade tensions. 
Should there be a split Congress with the Democrats controlling the House, and Trump 
does indeed focus on foreign and trade policy, volatility and uncertainty in the global 
economy could increase. This will clearly affect Europe. At the same time, with only two 
years left in office, the TACO (Trump always chickens out) phenomenon could be an 
increasing part of Europe’s considerations. The risk of an unwarranted economic 
accident increases as Trump’s policies become more aggressive vis-à-vis Europe and 
Europe’s own stance hardens (hoping for TACO and an end to Trump’s term in office). 
If there is a Democrat clean sweep, the sense that Trump’s time is coming to an end 
could be all the greater and, under this scenario, some European countries could choose 
to slow the pace of defence spending increases given domestic fiscal considerations. 
Regardless of the election outcome, China’s main response will be wait-and-see, as it will 
react to actual policy changes rather than just potential shifts within Congress. The main 
potential trigger points would be Taiwan arms sales, tariffs and other trade/investment 
restrictions. 
Personal ties between the two leaders appear good and if the Republicans retain control 
of Congress, Trump’s attention will be spread across a broader range of issues. This 
would make rocking the boat with China appear a little less likely. However, if President 
Trump is constrained by a Democrat-controlled Congress, then his focus will be 
channelled towards foreign and trade policy, meaning China could end up in the 
crosshairs again. The trade truce is set to expire in January, and it seems like both sides 
are not interested in going back to the mutually destructive peak tariff scenario we had 
in 2025, but it cannot be ruled out. If the Democrats win both the House and the Senate, 
it would send a signal to China that it is time to start looking beyond 2028, triggering a 
revaluation of its long-term US strategy.

### Page 16

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

16

The Ministry of the Economy and Finance in Bercy, Paris. Even if the current budget proposal is adopted, fiscal 
uncertainty will not disappear in France

France is in the eye of the storm. Its public finances have deteriorated significantly, the 
budget faces a difficult parliamentary process, and the presidential election offers no 
clear prospect of political stabilisation. Economic conditions provide little support; we 
expect GDP growth of only 0.3% in 2026 and 0.6% in 2027. 
France’s debt dynamics are particularly unfavourable. Over the past 15 years, its public 
debt ratio has risen significantly, while many other European countries have reduced 
theirs. French public debt reached 119% of GDP in mid-2026 and is expected to increase 
to 121.7% in 2027. Persistent primary deficits, rising interest payments and weak growth 
mean that debt will continue to rise without sustained fiscal adjustment. 
The budget would limit the deterioration, not stabilise debt 
The draft budget aims to reduce the public deficit to 5% of GDP in 2027, after 5.4% in 
2026 and 5.1% in 2025. This requires €43bn of new measures. Without them, the 
government estimates that the deficit would reach around 6.5% next year. 
This is a substantial adjustment and is consistent with France’s European expenditure 
trajectory. But a 5% deficit remains too high to stabilise debt, particularly with weak 
growth and a rising interest bill. Even if implemented in full, the budget would only limit 
the deterioration; the debt ratio would continue to rise. 
The budget is likely to pass, but concessions may be needed 
The parliamentary process can run until 17 December under the 70-day constitutional 
timetable. Without a majority in the National Assembly, the government is unlikely to 
secure approval in a normal vote. Our base case is that it uses Article 49.3 to adopt the 
budget without one, potentially after making concessions to ensure that a subsequent 
no-confidence motion fails. This could reduce the final fiscal adjustment. 
We, nevertheless, expect the government to survive. Opposition parties have little 
incentive to trigger another political crisis shortly before the presidential election, take 
Charlotte de Montpellier 
Senior Economist, France and Switzerland 
charlotte.de.montpellier@ing.com

France’s 2027 budget is only the first 
fiscal test  
France’s 2027 budget would prevent a sharp deterioration in the public finances, but it 
would not be enough to stabilise public debt. Its adoption would provide some relief, 
although fiscal and political risks are likely to remain well beyond next year's presidential 
election

### Page 17

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

17 
responsibility for the resulting instability and risk a further deterioration in the public 
finances. 
Alternatively, the government could let the deadline expire and impose its original 
budget by ordinance. It would first have to survive any pre-emptive no-confidence 
motion. Once the ordinances were adopted, however, the budget would remain in force 
even if the government subsequently fell. 
Failing either outcome, a special law would ensure continuity of the state. But the 
deadlock could last until the summer and, without corrective measures, cause a sharp 
deterioration in the public finances. 
France therefore faces another two months of budget uncertainty. Adoption would be a 
relative success, preventing the deficit from rising towards 6.5%. But concessions could 
dilute the adjustment, while even the original package would not stabilise debt. 
Fiscal uncertainty will persist in 2027 
Even if the budget is adopted, fiscal uncertainty will not disappear. The next government 
will still have to implement further reforms. Yet the presidential election on 18 April and 
2 May 2027 will probably be followed by legislative elections, which could again produce 
a fragmented National Assembly and make those reforms difficult to pass. 
There is also little clarity on the future fiscal strategy. Few prospective candidates have 
presented sufficiently detailed plans to stabilise public finances, and almost none appear 
willing to tackle pensions, despite their being the largest component of public spending 
and central to France’s structural fiscal problem. 
The National Rally, which leads first-round polls, has made fiscal consolidation a priority. 
But its plan, in our view, relies on very optimistic macroeconomic assumptions and 
difficult-to-deliver savings, as discussed in our assessment of its fiscal programme. The 
far-left La France Insoumise and its candidate, Jean-Luc Mélenchon, support cancelling 
part of the debt held by the central bank and pursuing an expansionary fiscal policy. 
There is even less visibility on who will emerge from the centre, the right and the rest of 
the left, or on their fiscal plans. 
The 2027 budget will be a critical first step in containing France’s fiscal deterioration. 
Whether that adjustment can be sustained and extended after the election remains 
much more uncertain.

### Page 18

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

18

ECB President Christine Lagarde

The ECB’s most powerful tools are the ones it has never used. They are the tools that the 
ECB developed after Mario Draghi’s ‘whatever it takes’ speech. Tools that showed 
‘whatever it takes’ was something markets could believe in. The Outright Monetary 
Transactions programme during the euro crisis and the Transmission Protection 
Instrument (TPI) after the pandemic both shared the same objective: to enable 
potentially unlimited purchases of government bonds if needed. 
Political and fiscal woes in France and the widening of the French spread have now 
raised the question of whether the ECB could intervene through TPI, which allows it to 
purchase securities issued by member states whose financing conditions have 
deteriorated for reasons not justified by country-specific fundamentals. However, before 
activating it, the Governing Council considers a cumulative set of criteria to determine 
whether the countries concerned are pursuing sound and sustainable fiscal and 
macroeconomic policies. In practice, this includes compliance with the EU fiscal 
framework, which would, at a minimum, require the proposed French budget to be fully 
adopted. 
The ECB has nevertheless left itself some discretion, stating that the intervention criteria 
“will be dynamically adjusted to the unfolding risks and conditions to be addressed”. This 
brings back memories of the famous “because it’s France” by former European 
Commission President Jean-Claude Juncker. Generally speaking, the ECB will be in a 
difficult position: intervening too quickly in support of France may be viewed 
unfavourably by other member states and interpreted as monetary financing of the 
debt of a country unwilling to follow the rules. That argues strongly against early 
intervention. Also, don’t forget that the leadership changes at the ECB over the next 
months (Schnabel, Lane, Lagarde) could also affect how the ECB views TPI. 
Given the challenges of justifying the activation of TPI at present, we expect the ECB to 
adopt a three-pronged approach to address the French crisis. 
Carsten Brzeski 
Global Head of Macro and Chief 
Economist, Eurozone, Germany, Austria 
carsten.brzeski@ing.de

The ECB's options in dealing with 
France's fiscal woes 
The bar for activating the ECB's bond-buying programme remains high. However, a 
further deterioration in France's situation could eventually force the ECB to demonstrate 
whether its 'whatever it takes' commitment still holds

### Page 19

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

19 
The first line of defence is a more dovish approach to policy rates. As bond markets are 
currently taking over the ECB’s job of inserting a more restrictive monetary stance on 
the economy, there is less need for the ECB to hike rates aggressively, or to hike rates at 
all. In fact, the ECB has already started to prepare this option, as ECB president Christine 
Lagarde recently noted that “while growth has been resilient, since our last meeting 
long-term interest rates have risen notably, which will slow growth and reduce pass-
through by more than projected in our September exercise". 
The second line of defence would be a covert return to bond purchases. The ECB could 
instead pause quantitative tightening temporarily and reinvest maturing bonds in its 
portfolio “flexibly”, sending a positive signal to bond markets. This had already happened 
during the Covid period. 
Last but not least, and in our view, the ultimate option in the case of more contagion in 
eurozone bond markets, the ECB would eventually trigger TPI. As long as France remains 
non-compliant with the European fiscal rules, the ECB might even consider purchasing 
bonds from almost all eurozone countries, except for France. 
All in all, the current French situation is simply not enough for the ECB to trigger TPI. 
There are other ways to tackle any unwarranted impact from higher bond yields on the 
broader eurozone economy. However, a further escalation could eventually force the 
ECB to prove whether its 'whatever it takes' commitment still holds.

While some parts of the US economy continue to thrive, others are feeling the strain

Growing divergences in US sector performance 
The US economy continues to perform strongly, although it masks growing divergences 
between sections of the economy. In terms of the consumer, high-income households 
continue to spend strongly, boosted by large wealth gains in recent years, while middle 
and lower incomes are being squeezed by slowing income growth and elevated 
inflation. This is underscored by the breakdown in the relationship between sentiment 
and spending, with household stress highlighted by declines in the saving ratio and 
rising loan delinquencies. The Federal Reserve’s Survey of Consumer Finances reported 
that 8.6% of US households now spend more than 40% of their income on debt 
obligations. 
University of Michigan consumer confidence & consumer spending growth YoY%

The job market sees similar bifurcation. Government, leisure and hospitality and private 
education and healthcare services are adding jobs while other sectors are seeing flat to

james.knightley@ing.com

Strong US growth masks growing splits 
High-income household spending and robust tech investment remain the key drivers of 
US growth. Higher borrowing costs are intensifying the stresses elsewhere, and this is 
prompting the Federal Reserve to take a gradual approach to policy tightening

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

modest declines in employment levels. One sector showing signs of revival is 
manufacturing, with job gains of 72,000 year-to-date after three consecutive years of 
declines. Again, performance varies, with advanced manufacturing output 
(electronics/technology, transport and aerospace, speciality chemicals, pharma and 
power) experiencing robust growth, while more commoditised manufacturing, where it 
is difficult to charge a premium for Made-in-America products, is seeing output fall. 
Then there is tech capex, which continues to soar amid the insatiable appetite to invest 
in data centres to facilitate growing AI demand. Power investment is also performing 
strongly, but outside of tech, we see little growth. 
Slow and steady from the Fed 
The growing split in performance is being intensified by higher borrowing costs, 
particularly higher Treasury yields, which are putting more pressure on interest rate-
sensitive sectors. The housing market is facing strife, with mortgage rates now above 
7.5% and housing transactions on par with the downturn experienced during the Global 
Financial Crisis. Auto sales are also cooling. 
This has raised questions about how effective monetary policy is right now in containing 
inflation. Federal Reserve interest rate increases won’t reopen the Strait of Hormuz, and 
they are probably not going to do much to restrain the big tech companies from 
spending aggressively on AI-related activities. However, it is increasingly hurting the 
weaker parts of the economy. 
Inflation fears to rapidly fade once a Middle East deal is done 
Fed officials have alluded to this risk, with some senior members suggesting they favour 
slow and steady tightening. We currently expect one more rate rise, which we expect to 
come at the December FOMC meeting. We don’t anticipate any shift in the US-Iran 
conflict ahead of the midterms (see our extended piece on what the election could 
mean), but with polls suggesting Democrats will at least gain control of the House, this 
will restrict the president’s ability to pass legislation, leaving him to focus on foreign 
policy after the election. Assuming we do see an agreement to improve Persian Gulf 
energy flows, this should significantly ease global inflation pressures, and we don’t think 
the Fed will go further. However, the risk is clearly tilted towards a longer-lasting conflict 
rather than one that is resolved imminently. This means we would not rule out an 
additional rate hike in the first quarter of 2027. 
Outside of energy and tech, inflation pressures are cooling. Housing is the largest 
component of the inflation basket by weight and the downturn here is increasingly likely 
to depress the shelter metrics. Furthermore, we are in a less onerous tariff regime than 
in 2025, while wage growth continues to cool given the low-hire, low-fire economy. 
So, when flows through the Strait of Hormuz normalise, we expect market concerns 
about inflation to recede. Any additional Fed rate hikes over and above what we expect 
are likely to be swiftly reversed once an agreement is reached.

Higher energy and borrowing costs continue to weigh on the eurozone economic outlook

October is the cruelest month 
October is budget season in Europe, when cash-strapped member states must show 
they can put their finances in order. France’s minority government has proposed a 
budget that curbs expenditure growth but would still fail to stabilise the debt ratio next 
year. With presidential elections due in April and May 2027 and street protests 
mounting, few parties are willing to back austerity. We still expect the government to 
invoke Article 49.3 to pass the budget without parliamentary approval, but tensions may 
rise first. 
As for the rest of the eurozone, contagion has already widened bond spreads in other 
highly indebted member states, tightening eurozone financial conditions. This will 
increase pressure on governments to engage in austerity measures, resulting in a less 
accommodative fiscal stance in 2027. 
Growth likely to slow temporarily 
Energy prices remain relatively high, while wage growth is failing to keep pace with 
inflation. Households drew on savings in the second quarter and may need to do so 
again to support spending. Yet signs of a weakening labour market and renewed falls in 
consumer confidence point to softer consumption growth in the coming quarters. 
Residential construction may also gradually feel the impact of higher bond yields. 
Business sentiment suggests GDP growth remained moderate in the third quarter, but 
strengthening headwinds are likely to weaken growth in the fourth quarter and the first 
quarter of next year. Following a very strong second quarter, artificially boosted by Irish 
GDP figures, we now expect average growth of 1.0% in 2026. Assuming energy prices 
normalise in 2027, growth should average 1.2% next year.

Second-round effects remain more moderate than in 2022-23 
Survey on selling price expectations

Source: LSEG Datastream

Second-round inflation effects remain moderate 
The eurozone's harmonised index of consumer prices (HICP) inflation rose to 3.8% in 
September, with underlying inflation at 2.5%. Energy prices are likely to lift the headline 
rate further in the coming months, while food prices, which are highly sensitive to 
energy costs, are also trending higher. Inflation is therefore likely to end the year above 
4%. 
At the same time, second-round effects remain muted. They may still emerge, but we 
do not expect a repeat of the full-blown inflation wave of 2022-23. Business surveys 
show that selling-price expectations have risen less than they did four years ago, 
suggesting firms have less pricing power as consumers turn more cautious. We expect 
inflation to fall back to an average of 2.5% next year. 
One last rate hike? 
We still expect one more European Central Bank rate hike, although higher bond yields 
are already doing much of the ECB’s work. Markets have moved towards our view, 
scaling back expectations from almost four increases to around one. Given the bond 
market unrest, we also expect the ECB to halt quantitative tightening before year-end, 
echoing earlier moves by the Federal Reserve and the Bank of England.

Trade ties may be calmer, but Beijing's global challenges are far from over

State Council meeting triggers new targeted policy support 
China's State Council meeting on 28 September called for stronger countercyclical policy 
adjustments. The following day, the People’s Bank of China cut the one-year pledged 
supplementary lending rate by 25bp to 1.5%. It also increased re-lending quotas for 
technology and equipment upgrades and for rural and small businesses. Beijing 
announced the first-ever nationwide mortgage interest subsidy for first-time 
homebuyers to boost the struggling property market. 
The package maintains an accommodative policy direction but relies on targeted tools 
and fiscal-monetary coordination rather than broad-based rate cuts. 
Overall, these moves are likely to offer modest support to investment and housing at a 
time when credit demand is likely to remain under pressure amid weak sentiment. 
There remains room for further support measures, such as policies to boost employment 
and incomes and other measures to boost domestic demand. Should economic 
momentum remain subdued into year-end, policymakers may respond with further 
fiscal stimulus and a broader easing of monetary policy. 
External trade ties stable for now, but risks remain 
President Xi’s visit to the US in September continued the “constructive strategic stability” 
framework agreed in May. It secured a short-term extension of the trade truce to 
January 2027, and $30bn of tariff cuts on both sides. 
It’s possible the leaders will meet again at the APEC summit in Shenzhen on 18-19 
November and at the G20 meetings in Florida on 14-15 December. 
Aside from the US, possible tensions with the EU have also grabbed the spotlight over 
the past month. A joint letter from German Chancellor Friedrich Merz and French 
President Emmanuel Macron calling for tougher trade tools signals increasing risk that 
we could see trade barriers come into effect. EU-China trade talks concluded with an

China’s inflation remains the exception to the rule 
We’ve seen inflation rise in many economies across the world. But prices in China have 
largely remained under control. Most worry about a return to deflation more than out-
of-control inflation. 
Two factors are curbing China’s inflation: food and housing. Together, these represent 
over half of China’s CPI basket. Food inflation has been kept low amid an extended 
downturn in China’s pork price cycle. Supply has been aided by advancements in hog 
megafarms, low feed prices, and general efforts to maintain high levels of food self-
sufficiency. Housing prices, meanwhile, have declined amid China’s multi-year property 
bust. 
On net, China’s inflation looks likely to end the year solidly in positive territory. We 
expect 0.9% CPI inflation in 2026, firmly positive after three years of flirting with 
deflation. However, it will once again fall short of the target, raising questions about 
whether China has successfully escaped deflation. While we believe the worst is likely 
behind us, downside risks remain. 
PPI inflation, on the other hand, averaged 2.0% in the first eight months of the year, 
peaking at 4.1% year-on-year in June. This suggests industrial prices have moved out of 
deflation, driven by upstream and select technology sectors. Yet this level is generally 
still a fair bit lower than in other Asian economies this year.
